1084. DANIEL LIVERMORE and JAMES WHEELER were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of March, at West Ham, Essex, I mare, price 50 s., the property of Edward Harrington.
EDWARD HARRINGTON . I am a cow-keeper, and live at Forert-gate, Wanstead, Essex—I had a little black mare, on the forest—I saw her there on the Monday previous to Saturday, the 21st of March, when I missed her, about seven o'clock in the morning—I had had her four or five months—I turned her out near my own home; but the last time I saw her, she was lying near Wanstead—on Saturday morning, the 21st, the patrol produced her to me—I had not missed her—I knew Livennore by sight, but not by name.
SAMUEL GUTTERIDGE . I am a Bow-street patrol. I saw Livermorne leading this mare, near the New Church at Stratford, about six o'clock, on the morning of the 21st of March—he was leading her towards London, by a halter, but no saddle—I asked where he brought it from—he said, from Mr. Carter's, a farmer at Epping, and he was going to take it to Perkins, of Tooting, for Carter—I asked if he had any note or direction to Mr. Perkins—he said, "No"—I asked which way he came from Epping—he said he came through the Bald-stag-gate—I asked if he had any turnpike-ticket—he said he had lost it—I knew he had not come that way, which excited my suspicions—I told him I suspected he had stolen the mare—he said, "Well, I cannot deceive you, I have"—he then said he had been dawn into it by another man, and described his dress to me and my partner, (Johnson,) who pursued Wheeler, from Livermore's description, and brought him back in about a quarter of an hour—I showed the mare to the prosecutor that morning, and he owned it.
DAVID JOHNSON . I am a Bow-street patrol. I was with Gutteridge—his
sevidence is correct—when Livermore described the other man, I went in pursuit towards London, and overtook Wheeler, at Bow, about two miles further on—when I got up to him, I asked him where he had come from—he said, "From the other side of Brentwood"—I said, "What way did you come?"—he said, "Through Romford"—I asked if he saw a man leading a horse on the road—he said yes, he did, and the man asked him the road to Lon-don, and he told him to turn round the corner—I asked him how the man was dressed—he said he had got a frock on—I had met a waggon, and asked him if he, had seen that waggon on the road—he said yes, he had, and he knew the waggon by the boy who was with it—I said, "It is clear to me you have not come through Romford, for that waggon came over Woodford Bridge"—I took him back to Livermore, and said to Livermore, "Is this the man?"—he said, "Yes, it is"—Wheeler said, "Well, you might have seen me before"—the same mare was afterwards shown to the prosecutor.
Wheeler. Q. Did not I tell you I was not acquainted with the road, and had got out of the road? A. You must be acquainted with the road, for I have seen you pass my house repeatedly—yon are well known about the country.
DANIEL WALLIS . I live at Wanstead. On Saturday, the 21st of March, I was up about ten minutes before five o'clock in the morning—I am a green-grocer, and keep mares on the forest—I was looking after my mares on the forest about five o'clock in the morning, and met two men with a black mare or horse, I could not tell which—the man in a fustian dress was leading her, and the one in a blue dress was walking by his side—the blue dressed one walked away towards a cottage—I suspected they had stolen the mare—I turned round, and they joined again—the man in a blue dress opened the gate, and let the other through—the prisoners are the two men.
SAMUEL GUTTERIDGE re-examined. Wheeler had on a large blue coat, and the other a fustian jacket.
Wheeler's Defence. I was not near the man—I passed him on the road, and afterwards stopped to ease myself—he got before me, and then I passed him again.
LIVERMORE— GUILTY .* Aged 40.
WHEELER— GUILTY . Aged 35
Transported for Life.
KENT LARCENIES.
Second Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18350406-1085]
1085. WILLIAM TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of March, at Charlton, Kent, 6 trusses of hay, value 1 l.; and 10lbs. weight of hay, value 1 s., the goods of James Strother.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ANTHONY STROTHER . I am the son of James Strother, who lives at Woolwich, in Kent—he has marshes at Charlton. I missed hay at various times, and in consequence of that, I communicated, on the 19th, with Warden, and gave him instructions—I went with him that evening to the Marshes, and found six trusses of hay bound, in the enclosed fence—Warden put into the stack three pieces of paper, and into each truss several pieces of paper—on the following morning we found the hay was gone—I went to the prisoner's house, and found the hay there—it was my father's hay, I have not the least doubt of it—the pieces of paper were found in it
—the prisoner is, I believe, a labouring gardener—he keeps a horse—we found the hay in his stable loose—not in trusses, as it was the night before—the moment I saw it I was convinced it was my father's, and at the botton of the shed were three distinct parts pf the bottoms of trusses, and in one as them I saw the paper.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When were the pieces of paper put into the trusses? A. At eight o'clock on Thursday night—I missed the hay at six o'clock next morning—the paper had writing on both sides, if I am not mistaken—he put the paper first into the stump of the hay, and nearly into the heart of the trusses—we found it in the prisoner's stable at four o'clock on Saturday afternoon—I did not look for any traces of hay—I should think there was from six to seven trusses of hay in the stable—it was nearly all loose, except three distinct parts of trusses—it is usual to open and shake it when it is dusty, but not six trusses at once—that would last one horse eighteen days.
COURT. Q. Was this dusty hay? A. No; it was the same quality as our hay—I should have known it to be the produce of that stack without the papers—there is a rank weed grows in the Marshes, and those weeds were amongst the hay.
CHARLES STEWART WARDEN . I am superintendent of the Woolwich police. I went with the prosecutor to the field on the 19th, and saw six trusses of hay in an enclosure, and part of a hay-stack—I took a slip of paper, containing my report-notes, and tore off three or four pieces—I put them into the uncut part of the stack, and put one piece into each truss—I went to the field next morning, about five o'clock—the trusses were gone, also one portion of the uncut—one of my tickets laid on the ground—I went and informed Mr. Strother—I made search in the town, and on Saturday morning I went to the field, and from the entrance of the field I traced tracks of hay to the prisoner's house—I called out, "Is Mr. Taylor here?" several times—I saw the stable door open—I opened it more, went in, and said, "Is Mr. Taylor here?"—nobody answered—I found the hay there—the prisoner's daughter came out and spoke to me—I went and told Mr. Strother that there was hay there, but I could not identify it—in the afternoon he went with me, and I found three of my tickets in it, which I had put in on Thursday night with my own handwriting upon them—the prisoner's son was on the premises, and said he had purchased the hay—I took him into custody, and the father came up to me—I said I should take him, and I believe Chittendale took him into custody—I am certain the papers are the same—they are my own handwriting.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go to the hay-stack on Friday morning? A. I did—I observed no tracks of hay then, as it was between day-light and moon-light—I could not see well enough—I saw some tracks on the Saturday between six and seven o'clock in the morning—I was searching in Woolwich on Friday for parties who were suspected—I walked from the entrance of the field to the prisoner's on Saturday morning, not from the hay-stack—I did not go further than the threshold of the stable when I called out, "Is Mr. Taylor here?"—nobody was with me then—I was at the stable door when I saw the daughter—I was coming from the stable door—I re-entered the stable again between six and seven o'clock that morning—I did not go up to the hay—but in the course of two minutes, I went into the stable and up to the hay—the daughter was then in the yard looking at us—I took up the hay, looked at it, and one of the police who was with me felt about and said, "There are no trusses here"—we told the
daughter we could not identify it, as we were is search of trusses, and I should report it to Mr. Strother—the policeman stood by the wall—I was not alone in the stable that morning—one of the police entered with me, when I told the daughter the purport of our errand—the policeman went in alongside the hay—it is a small stable—I found the papers about four o'clock in the afternoon—I did not feel myself justified to search the hay in the morning, as I did not know it was the hay lost—the daughter told me the hay could not be stolen, for it was bought on Tuesday morning; and the brother told me the same, and so did the father—the prisoner did not come up and say, "What are you doing with my son?"—he had some conversation with the policeman, and made some remark about that hay—I said I shall take you before the Magistrate; and both walked in my company.
WILLIAM CHITTENDEN . I am a policeman. I went to the prisoner's house and examined the hay; and found two pieces of paper in it, which Warden has—I saw a ladder in the yard, which I had seen on the prosecutor's premises, just against the hay-rick.
Cross-examined. Q. Where were you when you saw the ladder against the hay rick? A. Against the enclosure—it was on Friday morning, the 20th of March—it was the same ladder, by the appearance of iter—it has about seven or eight rounds—it was decidedly the same—I examined it—I was looking at the hay-binder for a quarter of an hour—the top and bottom round was plain; it is rather uncommon to have them so at both top and bottom—I found the first piece of paper in part of a truss—I took care of it, and produced it before the Magistrate—I went with Warden to the stable—I was outside when he first went into the gates, but followed him afterwards—I heard him calling for Taylor, but he was then out of my sight for about half a minute at the outside—the hay binder has ackowledged that he borrowed the ladder of the prisoner—it is not the prosecutor's.
C. S. WARDEN re-examined. My writing is on both sides of the paper—C. S. W., my initials was written after finding it—the writing on the other side was written before—it alludes to the conduct of two watchmen which I noticed—I found these papers in the hay, and I had put them into the hay.
Prisoner's Defence. I am innocent.
HENRY TAYLOR . I am the prisoner's son. I had purchased a quarter of a load of hay in Smithfield-market, on Tuesday, the 17th of March; that was nine trusses—I took it home—my sister opened the gate, and let me in with it—I then put it into the stable—when my father came home, I told him the hay was a little dusty—I paid 1 l. 5 s. for it—he told me to take the bands off, shake the hay up, and throw a little salt in it—I did so—we had one horse—it was out from Tuesday till Saturday—some of the hay was gone—I was not at home on Thursday aad Friday, and did not see the stable till Saturday—there was then no hay in the stable, but the hay I had bought—when the officers came on the Saturday I was in-doors lying on the bed—my sister called me up—I told the officers whew I got the hay.
MR. DOANE. Q. You bought the hay on Tuesday morning? A. Yes—I went to the Borough-market with a load of greens, and want from thence to Smithfield-market, and bought the hay of a perfect stranger—I did not ask his name—we have only one horse—I buy very little hay—I never bought any at Smithfield before—I bought a quarter of a load—they will sell that quantity in Smithfield-market—that was the first time I had been there to buy hay this season—I do not suppose there
was above half of a truss left in the stable when I left on the Tuesday, and went to buy this—I was working for Mr. Glinton on Wednesday—I slept at my father's house on Wednesday night—I worked at home in my father's garden on Thursday, and slept in his bed at night—I was coming to London market on Friday night—I never said I was not at home on Thursday and Friday—I got up at six o'clock on Friday morning—the hay I bought was dusty, and the hay that was found on my father's premises was dusty—it was the hay I had brought home—it was not sweet.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Does your father send greens to market? A. Yes—he slept at home on Thursday night.
COURT. Q. You never bought hay before? A. Not in Smithfield-market—we generally come to Smithfield to buy it cheap—the man I bought it of had about a load and a half in his cart—he threw nine trusses off the cart—I did not notice any little pieces of paper amongst it—hay was 5 l. a load—a man bought it before me, with intent to sell it again—I think he was a corn-dealer—I never asked him for any written document—I met a neighbour of ours on the road, who saw me take it away—he lives about half a mile from us.
JURY. Q. It being a long distance from Smithfield, at what watering-house did you stop on the road? A. At the World turned upside down, and gave my horse a drop of water—I did not see any ostler—I had not left the house long before I met the witness and spoke to him.
C. S. WARDEN re-examined. The prisoner was allowed by the Magistrate to leave the office on Saturday, and come on Monday—he gave two sureties for that.
TABITHA TAYLOR . I am the prisoner's daughter. I remember my father and brother being taken into custody—I remember, some days before that, my brother bringing home some hay about one o'clock—it was put into the stable—my father was gone from home, but when he came home at night he examined it, and said it was rather dusty, and told my brother to shake it—I saw it shaken by my brother in the stable—my father slept at home on Thursday night, and my brother too—the superintendent came on Saturday morning, about seven o'clock—I heard the dog bark, and went out—I saw the man in the stable by himself, examining the hay—I asked him what he wanted—he came out of the stable immediately—the two other policemen were at a distance from the stable—he asked where my father was—I said at work at Charlton, and he would return at dinner-time, which he did—the policeman said he had been informed there was some hay stolen, and it had been traced to my father's premises—I denied it—I said my brother bought it in London, and I had let him in at the gate with it—the policeman told me that this was not the hay—he came again about three o'clock in the afternoon, and asked if my father was at home—I said not, but if he had come at dinner-time, as I told him, he would have seen him—I said my brother was at home, and I would call him—he came to him, and they went out together—my brother had been lying down—he went into the yard to them—I do not know what passed between them.
MR. DOANE. Q. Your brother bought some hay on Tuesday, and you let him in? A. Yes; about one o'clock, in the middle of the day—(my brother went away immediately to Woolwich, as they had taken his hay)—my brother did not go very often to market to buy hay—he sometimes went with greens, and then if we were short, he would buy it—he sometimes bought hay at Greenwich and Woolwich, and sometimes in London
—he had gone to London three or four times, or more, to buy hay this year—he might have gone six times—I let him in when he came back—I cannot tell when hay is dusty—we generally get up early; sometimes at six, and sometimes later—I cannot recollect who got up first, on Friday morning—I got up between six and seven o'clock—I am sure I was not up before five o'clock—I am never up so soon as that.
WILLIAM SAVAGE . I am a market-gardener, and live in Lower-road, Charlton. I have a sample of weeds here—I got it from 'Squire Bedford's hay-stack, "yesterday morning—it is a common weed, which will grow any where—I can pick it out of my own garden—it will grow in any soil—I have known the prisoner five years—he always bore a good character.
Q. Did you ever go to the 'Squire's hay-stack before? A. Never; I went because the prisoner asked me for a character—I put my hand in the stack and pulled the weed out.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Had you heard that Mr. Strother had sworn to the hay, by a particular kind of weed? A. No, I had not; I brought the weed here to show it.
JURY. Q. What made you take that weed out the rick? A. The same sort of weed was produced to the Court, and Mr. Strother said it grew on his marshes.
JOSEPH BLAND . I am a labourer. I remember meeting young Taylor in the Kent-road, on the 17th of March, between the Dun Cow, and the Little Castle, close by the turnpike—the World turned upside down, is nearer London—there is a trough for horses to drink there—he had some hay in his cart—seven or eight trusses—I did not count them—I have known the prisoner four years—he is a hard-working, industrious man.
MR. DOANE. Q. Were you in any employment at the time? A. I was going to Church-street, Spitalfields, with a load of lime, for Mr. Glinton—I have met the prisoner's son with his fruit-cart—I do not know that I ever met him with hay above twice—this was on the 17th of March, St. Patrick's Day—I was in town the day before—I come up almost everyday in the week—he spoke to me as he passed—I think there was a load of straw in his cart besides—I cannot say whether there was any turnips or not.
COURT. Q. Has any body been talking to you about the World turned upside down, before you came into Court? A. No, nobody—I met him about eleven o'clock, as near as I can say—Charlton is between five and six miles from London.
MR. STROTHER re-examined. These weeds are similar to what I spoke of—this was not dusty hay at all, when it was found on the premises, nor was my hay dusty—it had been got up last summer, which was a very fine season—whatever may be done to hay it will not get dust out.
(John Boyd, master gardener, Charlton, give the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY . Aged 46.— Transported for Seven Years.
Second Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18350406-1086]
1086. SARAH GARDENER was indicted for feloniously and burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Pickering, about the hour of eleven o'clock in the night of the 28th of March, at Woolwich, with intent to steal, and feloniously and burglariously stealing therein, 1 watch-guard, value 6 d.; 1 tumbler, value 4 d.; 1 breast-pin, value 6 d.; 1 ear-drop, value 6 d.; 1 wine-glass, value 6 d.; 1 jug, value 1 d.; 2 shillings; and 6 sixpences, the goods and monies of Sarah Treasure.
SARAH TREASURE . I am single, and lodge at No. 11, Union-buildings, in the house of William Pickering, who lives in the house—it is in the parish of Woolwich, in Kent. On Saturday, the 28th of March, I left my room about half-past eleven o'clock at night—I shut the door too, and latched it, but did not lock it—I am sure the latch caught—I heard it—I was gone about half an hour—I left the street door shut—there are other lodgers in the house—when I returned I found the street door shut—I found my own door wide open, and the prisoner in the room—it was dark—there was no light there—she did not lodge in the house—it was dark; but I knew her by her short sleeve frock—I asked her three times what she did in my room—she made me no answer—I am sure it was her—the ran out of the room, and went to her mother's door, which is a back door adjoining mine—I saw her go there, and went into her mother's room after her—I got a light, and saw her there—her mother was in bed—I asked her if she would give me a light—she said, "Yes"—I went into my room with a light, and missed these things off the mantel-piece—I went to her, and accused her of the robbery—she used very infamous words, and said she was not guilty—I said to her, "Sarah Gardener, you are the girl who has got these things"—she said she was not the girl—I went straight to the watch-house—Alsford, the constable, came with me—the prisoner was in her mother's room when the constable came—the constable searched her, and took her to the watch-house—before the constable came, she ran from her mother's door out into the street—I ran after her, and saw her fling all the articles away except a half-crown—she threw them against the palings, and I found them there directly—Harvey was picking them up as the constable and I were coming along—while we were at supper, Alsford brought in a little jug, and I said, "It belongs to me"—I do not know what she threw down at the time—I heard them fall—Harvey took them up.
THOMAS HARVEY . I am a coal-porter, and live in the same house as the prosecutrix. On the 28th of March, I came home at night about half-past twelve o'clock—I got a light, in consequence of what was told me and found four sixpences and one shilling in the passage; and an ear-ring, a button, and a farthing—that was in the passage of our house, very near the prisoner's mother's door—I found this property in the house—I went into the street—there are iron railings there—I found there a tumbler, a watch-guard, a pin, and a ball of worsted—I gave them to the constable—I saw the prisoner brought into the house by Earney.
JOHN ALSFORD . I am a watchman of Woolwich. On Saturday, the 28th of March, from information I received from the prosecutrix, I went with her to No, 11, Union-street, and received from Harvey a tumbler, guard-chain, four sixpences, one shilling, a pin, and an ear-drop—the prisoner had been drinking, but was collected enough to answer all my questions—she did not appear intoxicated—after taking her to the cage, I returned, and found this jug where I had seen Harvey pick up the articles.
JURY. Q. In what state was the prosecutrix? A. Quite sober—if she had been drinking I could not perceive it.
JACOB EARNEY . I am a labourer, and live with Treasure. I returned home on Saturday, at nearly twelve o'clock—when I got into the passage I shut the street door—I stood against the door of my room, which is on
the ground floor—it was open, and Treasure went in—the prisoner ran up against me, and knocked me nearly backwards—she gave me a great deal of ill language, and abused me—I heard her run along the passage, and heard some money drop—she ran into her mother's room; then ran out of the room into the street—I followed her, and heard her throw something against the railings—I caught hold of her—she scratched my face, and fell down—her mother and another woman came—we carried her into her mother's room, and the watchman came—I had had no quarrel with her—I lodge with the prosecutrix on the ground floor—mine is the first room in the passage—I found the door open, and the prisoner ran out—the property belongs to Treasure—none of it is mine—she does not support me—I have lived with her two or three years—Pickering occupies the room right opposite mine, and lives there with his family—he sleeps in the house—I pay the rent to him—I do not know of any quarrel between the prisoner and Treasure.
Prisoner. There are no railings facing the house. Witness. They are about twenty yards up the street—I did not beat or ill-use her—I went to catch hold of her frock behind, and she scratched my face and fell down—I did not see her bloody—I did not strike her.
SARAH TREASURE re-examined. These are all my property, and are worth about 5 s. I had no quarrel with the prisoner—I am certain I was sober—I had had one glass of gin with the prisoner when I went out to market, about half-past eight o'clock—I met her outside the street door, and we went down the town together—I parted with her about half-past eleven—I went home and put my market things away, and left her in the street—I went to get some corn-beef for supper—I was gone about half an hour I think—I went about one hundred yards—Jacob was going to partake of the supper—married people live in the house—I believe the prisoner is single, and so am I—I felt the door catch when I pulled it to—it if to near the street door, I always see that it is fast—the street door was only latched; it is common to all the lodgers—my door if kept on the latch, unless I only go into the yard.
Prisoner. Different people come out of the house at all hours of the night—it is a very bad house—the street door was open until three o'clock in the morning.
JACOB EARNEY re-examined. I came home in company with the prosecutrix—she had come to the Coach and Horses public-house after me—I had left the lodging about seven o'clock.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to my mother's about seven o'clock on Saturday evening—I left her house to go down the town—as I went out at the street door Treasure called to me, "Where are you going? stop a bit"—I waited for her while she put on her bonnet and shawl—she came out, and we went to High-street—we met a woman whom she knew, and we went into a public-house, and had a quartern of gin—we went into four or five different houses, and had gin and ale—I was in liquor, and so was she—that man used me dreadfully—but I do not believe I am guilty—I cannot remember any thing about it—when I found myself in the cage, I asked as watchman what I was there for; he said, Treasure had given me into custody—I am told Jacob Earney had brutally ill-used me.
JOHN ALSFORD re-examined. Earney appeared sober when he cam—I saw him holding the prisoner in the house—it was done with violence—I did not see any thing of the transaction myself but her nose was bleeding at the time.
(James Maxfield, of Union-street, Woolwich, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY of stealing only. Aged 19.— Confined Three Months.
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18350406-1087]
1087. GEORGE SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of March, 1 waistcoat, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Samuel Hitchcock.
SAMUEL HITCHCOCK . I am a slop-seller, and live in Broadway, Deptford. On the evening of the 17th of March, I saw the prisoner near my shop window—I saw him go away very quick, which excited my suspicions—I looked at the window and missed a waistcoat, and in about ten minutes afterwards, it was produced to me, with the prisoner in custody.
WILLIAM DYKE . I am a policeman. On the evening of the 17th of March, I saw the prisoner offering a waistcoat for sale at the Feathers, about one hundred yards from the prosecutor's—I took him into custody with it.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. As I was going home, a lad came up to me and said, "Do you want to buy a waistcoat?" I said, "No; but I know a young man who wore one very much like it"—I went to show it to the man at the Feathers—I asked him if he wanted one—he said no—the landlord said, "Do not bring things here for sale"—I came out directly, and the policeman took me—he shook me, and would not give me leave to speak a word—the boy ran away then, and he was seen the same night offering it for sale.
GUILTY. Aged 21.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor. — Confined Three Months.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18350406-1088]
1088. MARY HACKETT was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of March, 1 half-crown, and 2 shillings, the monies of Michael Robert Jenkins, her master.
MICHAEL ROBERT JENKINS . I keep the White Hart Inn, Stockwell-street, Greenwich. The prisoner has been my household-servant for about two months—I missed money on various occasions—in one week I lost 9 l. from a drawer—I marked four half-crowns and twenty shillings on the 4th of March, which I put into my till about eleven o'clock in the evening—I went into a room opposite the bar, where I could see through a window what took place in the bar—I saw the prisoner (after watching her mistress to see that she did not see her) open the till, and put her hand in—I waited where I was, and then saw the prisoner go to a table next to where her mistress was, take a bunch of keys, and walk to the private drawer, where we keep notes and gold—she opened the drawer with a key and put her hand in—at that moment I went to her and said, "Now I have detected you; I have long suspected you, and now I have found you"—I called Mr. Fagan, and told the prisoner to give up all the money she had taken from the till—she at first denied that she had taken any, and then said, "It was only half-a-crown"—I then took the other out of the till, and told Mr. Fagan it was marked, and what she had on her was marked in the same way—we found two shillings and half-a-crown short, and they were found on the prisoner.
JAMES FAGAN . I was in the house. I saw the prisoner produce the half-crown and two shillings, which had the same mark as that in the till.
Prisoner. I throw myself on the mercy of the Jury.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years.
Second Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18350406-1089]
1089. WILLIAM DEAN was indicted for embezzlement.
THOMAS SMITH . I am a baker, and live in Stockwell-street, Greenwich. The prisoner has been my journeyman for some years—it was his duty to receive monies and to account for them every day—I have my ledger here, in which my wife and daughter generally enter the accounts—I have not received 3 s. 9 d. of the prisoner, as received by him on the 19th of January, nor 4 s. 4 1/2 d. on the 26th, nor are there any entries of them in the ledger—I have seen the bills which are receipted by him, but the money has not been paid to me—he left me on the 15th of February—I have received this letter, which I believe to be in his writing ( reads)—"London, 15th of February, 1835. Sir, I have taken the liberty of writing to you, to tell you I am very sorry for what has taken place, and to assure you I will not wrong you of sixpence, if you will not proceed against me, for the sake of my wife and child, as I have a place in view in London, and I will pay you half my wages every week. I have sent you an account of what money I have received, as near as I possibly can; and I hope you will be merciful to me, as I expect my wife to be confined every day; and if you will be so kind as to let her know your determination, as I shall send to her by the same post, for she does not know where I am at present. I am, &c. W. DEAN."
CATHERINE SMITH . I am the wife of John Smith—he is a waterman—I paid the prisoner on the 19th of January 3 s. 9 d., and on the 26th of January, 4 s. 4 1/2 d.—I have the bills and receipts here, which he gave me.
MARGARET SMITH . I am the prosecutor's wife. The prisoner never paid me these sums.
MARGARET SMITH , Jun. I am the prosecutor's daughter. The prisoner never paid me these sums.
JURY to MR. SMITH. Q. What did the prisoner have a-week? A. Fif-teen shillings, and his bread and flour—I had given instructions to the police respecting this, two or three days before I received the letter.
GUILTY. Aged 37.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18350406-1090]
1090. EDWARD GRANT NOSWORTHY was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of March, 2 boxes, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 handkerchief, value 2 s.; 4 brushes, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 comforter, value 4 d.; 1 needle-case, value 2 d.; 1 printed book, value 3 s. 6 d.; 1 humming-top, value 1 s.; the goods of Thomas Josiah Morris; and 3 boxes, value 6 d.; 3 printed books, value 6 d.; 4 gloves, value 4 d.; 2 brushes, value 9 d.; 1 telescope, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 bag, value 2 d.; and 1 handkerchief, value 1 d.; the goods of John George Boorman.
MESSRS. ADOLPHUS and CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS JOSIAH MORRIS . I shall be fourteen years old next September—I belong to the Royal Hospital School, at Greenwich—I had a box there, which I saw safe on the 16th of March, under the arches in the school, where the boys usually keep their little boxes—it contained the articles stated.
MICHAEL ELLIS . I am a chimney-sweeper, and live at Greenwich. The prisoner wasin my service—I was employed to sweep the chimneys at the Royal Naval Asylum—I sent the prisoner and some other boys to sweep them, about the 17th of March—I afterwards had a complaint from the captain of the Asylum, and in consequence of what he said, I went into
the room where the prisoner and some other boys slept—I examined a box, which the prisoner said was his, and which has been claimed by one of the prosecutors—the box was locked—the prisoner gave me the key—it contained these articles—the prisoner then said he brought the boxes and the articles from the Naval Asylum in his sack—he and the property were taken before the Magistrates.
JOHN GEORGE BOORMAN . I am thirteen years old. I am in the Asylum school—the green box is mine—I saw it under the arches safe on the 16th of March—I missed it on the 17th—it was locked—it contained clothes-brushes, tooth-brushes, a telescope, some books, and other things; this is the box and the property.
GUILTY.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor. — Confined Three Days and Whipped.
His master promised to take him again.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18350406-1091]
1091. JANE JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of March, 1 Bible, value 2 s.; 1 umbrella, value 1 s.; 1 table-cloth, value 2 s.; 2 napkins, value 1 s.; 2 pair of stockings, value 3 s.; 1 petticoat, value 2 s. 6 d.; and 1 shift, value 2 s.; the goods of Ann Parrott.
ANN PARROTT . I live in Ship and Billet-row, Greenwich. The prisoner and her mother came to lodge at my house on the 18th of December—on the 17th of March, I went out at half-past nine in the morning, to go to Bexley—I left Elizabeth M'Kenzie and the prisoner in the house—the prisoner's mother had gone away on the Monday before—I had a box in my house, containing the greater part of the property stolen—I found on the 18th of March, that it had been opened, and the articles gone; and a pair of stockings from another box up stairs.
MARY DUNFORD . I am the wife of Henry Dunford, and live at Greenwich. On the 17th of March, the prisoner came to our house, and brought an umbrella, a Bible, a table-cloth, and two napkins—she asked me to purchase them—I asked if they were her own—she said, "No," but they belonged to the person who sent her with them—I gave her 1 s. 4 d. for the Bible and umbrella, and 1 s. for the table-cloth and napkin.
Prisoner's Defence. M'Kenzie gave me the things—I did not know they were stolen.
ANN PARROTT . M'Kenzie went away the same evening, and she did not return—the prisoner went out, but I went and found her—I could not find M'Kenzie.
NOT GUILTY .
Second Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18350406-1092]
1092. JOHN BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 3 glass cruets, value 4 s., the goods of John Roberts.
JOHN ROBERTS . I live in High-street, Deptford, and deal in earthenware. On the 28th of March, I heard a noise in my shop—I went into the shop and saw the prisoner—he asked me if a gentleman at Blackheath, named Foster, had ordered some decanters—I said, "No"—he said, a gentleman of that name was coming, and he just called to put me on my guard—he then went away, and I missed these cruets from a stand on the chair—I followed, and took him with them in his pocket.
(The prisoner pleaded poverty.)
GUILTY. Aged 34.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor and
Jury. — Confined Five Days.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18350406-1093]
1093. JOHN PARISH was indicted for embezzlement.
GEORGE BANHAM . I live at Montpelier-vale, Blackheath, and am in partnership with my father-in-law. The prisoner was in our employ—on the evening of the 13th of March, I marked some trusses of hay—I saw the same trusses safe on the morning of the 14th, which was Saturday—I concealed myself in the stable, and about seven o'clock the prisoner came into the stable, fed his horses, and then sat down on a truss of straw—some time afterwards, a man, whom I could not see, came to the door, and the prisoner said, "Come along, I just want one dram this morning"—he asked, the man if he had a handkerchief—he said he had, and he could have a little bit that morning—he went to the manger, but whether he took any thing or not I cannot say—the prisoner said he had not got much that morning, for his mistress had been peeping through that b—y hole—they then went away—I went for an officer, but could not find one—I went home, and the officer came some minutes after—I said nothing to the prisoner that morning, but he went through his work as usual—in the evening I went to the stable to see that every thing was safe, and missed two trusses of hay; but one of them I had ordered him to take to a customer—I went and got a search-warrant—we then went to Mrs. Loft's, where I found the truss of hay I had missed, with a stick in it, which I had put—I had directed him to take a truss to Mrs. Bevis', but not to Mrs. Loft's—it was his duty to sell hay; but when he did, he was to bring me the money for the hay so sold—he sold hay to different people—after he was in custody, he said he had trusted Mrs. Loft for four trusses of hay—I had not had the money.
Cross-examined by MR. MAGUIRE. Q. Was he allowed to trust any one who came for hay? A. I always cautioned him against trusting any one without my knowledge—he sometimes accounted to me for hay the evening he sold it, and sometimes not till the Saturday night—he has sometimes given my wife money for hay—I cannot swear that he had not given her the money for this.
LUCY LOFT . My husband is confined to his room. I dealt with the prisoner—I had a truss of hay of him on the 14th of March, which was to come to 3 s.—I did not pay him for it—I had fifteen trusses of him, and paid 7 s. off them.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350406-1094]
1094. JANE SHAWL was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 1 pair of shoes., value 1 s., the goods of Mary Smith.
MARY SMITH . I am a widow, and live in the New-road, Woolwich. On the 4th of April the prisoner came to me and asked me to buy a pair of shoes—she said she wanted 1 s. for them—I said that would not do—she said, "Will you give 6 d.?"—she then took up a pair of shoes from my table, and said, "What are these?"—I said, "A shilling"—she staid some time, and I turned to the fire, and she flew out of the shop with my shoes—I followed her as well as I could—she was taken and brought back with these shoes in her hand—they are mine.
SAMUEL NICHOLLS . I am constable of Woolwich. I took the prisoner about half an hour afterwards—I found these shoes in her bundle, and another pair of her own—she said she took then by mistake, and asked forgiveness.
Prisoner's Defence. I have no father. I ran away from my place that morning, as I had but 6 d. a week.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18350406-1095]
1095. THOMAS WILLIAMS and THOMAS PITMAN were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of March, 1 purse, value 6 d.; 1 half-crow, and 3 shillings; the goods and monies of John Clark, from his person.
JOHN CLARK . I am a gardener, and live at Bexley-heath. I went to the Crown and Masons' Arms, at Woolwich, on the 7th of March—I went into the tap-room between three and four o'clock—I remained there till between seven and eight o'clock, but was not drinking all the time—I had a leather purse with me, with a half-crown and three shillings in it, and some papers—I had taken it out to take a shilling out of it, about five o'clock, and I left 5 s. 6 d. in it—the two prisoners came in while I was there—they sat at the other end of the room, and then stood before the fire, and after that Williams came and sat by the side of me—I cannot say at what time that was—there was no clock in the room—I had a quartern of gin and peppermint—I put my hand to feel for my purse, and could not find it—I think it was then between five and six o'clock—that was about an hour after I had put the purse into my pocket—Charlotte Wheeler had been sitting by me—I was quite sober—the purse has not been found, but the constable found some money.
Williams. Q. Whose money was this? A. It was my mother's—I had slept at my mother's the night before—I got up about five o'clock in the morning—I left my mother about twelve o'clock—I am sure of that—I had 7 s. when I came away; 6 s. of my mother's, and 1 s. of my own—I stopped atone place on the road, where I went in to order some seeds, and two papers were put into my purse—I did not call for any refreshment till I got to the public-house at Woolwich—that was not in my way home—I am certain I had nothing to drink from the time I left home till I got to that house—I had been in that house before—I did not know the girls—I was in company with a young woman—we were talking about one thing and another, and we had one game at cards, or it might be two or three—we played for a quartern of gin and peppermint—I paid for one; I put my hand into my pocket to pay for the second, and could not find the purse—I had played a game before, that I won—I did not see you take my purse—I paid 5 d. for the gin and peppermint there, and 2 d. for a pint of beer, and I had changed a shilling before I went into that house—I might be an hour going from my house to the public-house; but I had called at a person's on Shooter's-hill, and stopped there about half an hour, and I called at one shop beside, where I stopped a quarter of an hour, or not so long—I was at no other place till I got to the public-house—I do not recollect dropping something, and saying it was a shilling, and picking up a half-penny—I did not swear before the Magistrate that I paid for two quarterns—I won one game, and the other two I lost—I paid for one quartern, and the other I could not pay for.
SARAH ANN TRACEY . I am single, and reside at Woolwich. I went to the Crown and Masons' Arms on the 7th of March, about two or three o'clock, with Charlotte Wheeler—I saw the prisoners there, in company; they had a pint of beer, and drank together—I saw the prosecutor there; a young woman sat by him, and they played at cards—he called for a quartern of gin and peppermint—I fetched it in, and asked him for the
money—he said, "Stop a minute, and I will give it you"—he sat upon the tail of his coat—he got up, and before he could put his hand into his pocket, Williams, the prisoner, put his hand into Clark's pocket and took out a red leather purse—the seat is not close to the wall—he put his hand behind him, and let the purse fall between the seat and the wall—I heard it drop—there was a piece of chalk on the table—he threw that down—he stooped down and passed the purse from where he let it down, over to Pitman, who took it, and walked away out of the house, and Williams went after him—I gave the alarm—the constable brought them back—some money was found on Williams, but no purse—when Williams was in the tap-room, he held up his finger to me, and shook his head—I saw Clark take out his purse when he first came in, and take a shilling out of it.
Williams. Q. How many games of cards did prosecutor play? A. It is out of my power to say—I did not drink any of the liquor—I believe two quarterns of gin and peppermint were brought in—I fetched in one, and Clark had not the money to pay for it, because you took the purse out of his pocket, and let it down behind you—I did not see Clark pay for the other quartern—I saw him take a shilling ont of his purse, and pay for a pint of beer, when he first came in—I did not see you open the purse, nor take any money out—I did not see the prosecutor drop a shilling from his hand, and look for it on the ground—I was not doing any thing when I saw you take the purse—I told Clark and the landlord, of it—I happened to be in the house, and fetched in the quartern of gin and peppermint—I saw a paper in the purse—I did not tee you take it out.
JURY. Q. Was the prosecutor sober? A. Yes.
CHARLOTTE WHEELER . I was in the public-house, playing at cards with the prosecutor—I had some gin and peppermint with him—I left my seat, and Williams took my seat by the side of the prosecutor—I saw him take the prosecutor's purse out of his pocket, and put it down behind the seat—I heard it drop—Pitman then went out, and Williams followed him—I told the landlord, and he went and brought them back.
Williams. Q. When I was taken, was not you in company with me? A. Yes; that was after the prosecutor lost his money—I met you, and kept you in conversation purposely—I did not ask you to go home and take tea with me—you asked me to let you go home, and I said, "Stop a minute"—when I am at home, I sleep with my father and mother, at East Harding, in Surrey—I had lived at Cannon-row, Woolwich—I did not sleep there every night—the first night I slept there, I paid for my bed—I am single, and work for my living—I did not live at the public-house at all—I cannot tell how many games of cuds I played, nor how much gin and peppermint I lost.
COURT. Q. Did you say before the Magistrate, that Williams said, "If I get my freedom I will mark you for it?" A. Yes, he did say so.
Williams. If the money was summed up which they say he paid, probably the amount would not correspond with the amount which he states was in the purse.
JOHN CLARK re-examined. Q. What money had you from your mother? A. Six shillings, and 1 s. of my own—I changed 1 s., and spent 9 d. at the seed-shop—I had 6 s. 3 d. when I went into the public-house—I paid 2 d. for the pint of beer, and had 10 d. change—I had some halfpence at the time I changed, but I did not feel in that pocket—I had then 6 s.; 5 s. 6 d. of it was silver, and was in the purse.
Williams. Q. How many quarterns did you pay for? A. One, and a pint of beer—when you came back, I was feeling in my pocket, and I found five or six pennyworth of halfpence in my right-hand pocket—my purse was a red leather one.
SAMUEL NICHOLLS . I am a constable. I received information, and took the prisoners—I found on Williams, one half-crown, five shillings, and four sixpences, in silver, and 1 1/4 d. in copper, all loose in one pocket—I found nothing on Pitman.
Williams. If I had time, I could bring forward a man who was before the Magistrate, who would say, on his oath, that one witness contradicted another; that the two females swore positively that I took it, and then that the other took it and gave it to me; and when I asked the witnesses questions, the Magistrate told me I had no right to ask questions. Witness. Williams examined the witnesses at great length.
Williams's Defence. I went into the house—I changed half-a-crown—I paid for a pint of beer—the landlord examined the half-crown for five or six minutes—there were about a dozen females of loose character there—I had 2 s. 4 d. change—I think the landlord ought to be here, for he was the very man who took us—I stopped there, as the card-playing took my attention—I was in the town selling my goods, and it begun to rain—I went into the house—I understand Tracey has been confined.
SARAH ANN TRACEY . No, I have not—I came from Colchester, which is my native place—I did not drink any beer—you cannot prove that I was ever at Chelmsford.
JURY. Q. Were you acquainted with Wheeler? A. No; I had seen her, but not spoken to her.
WILLIAMS— GUILTY . Aged 24.— Transported for Life.
PITMAN— GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18350406-1096]
1096. MARIA HOWARD was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 2 loaves of bread, value 1 s., the goods of Robert Standford.
ROBERT STANDFORD . I am a baker, and live in Church-street, Greenwich. On the 4th of April, the prisoner came into the shop with her mother—her mother bought a half-quartern loaf—she went out of the shop, and the prisoner followed her—I then missed two 41b. loaves which had stood near to where the prisoner was—I followed her, and found them on her.
Prisoner. I did not go out of the shop. Witness. Yes; she was just leaning upon the window outside.
NOT GUILTY .
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

1097. JAMES THOMAS RICHARDS, ROBERT DILLIMAN , and THOMAS OGDEN were indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of March, 1 till, value 2 s.; 60 pence, and 160 halfpence; the goods and monies of William Francis.
William Francis. I am a publican, and live in Thames-street, Dept-ford. On the 22nd of March I missed the till from under the counter in the bar, also some books, and about 12 s. in copper money, but no silver—there were different little keys in the till, but nothing of any consequence—I saw the prisoner, Richards, in my house about four o'clock, and I
lost the till between eight and nine o'clock—I had given, Richards 1 s. for a shilling's worth of copper.
Cross-examined by MR. MAHON. Q. How many persons had been in your house that evening? A. A great many—I do not know when I had seen my till last—I do not know exactly what I had there; I only judge by the bulk.
COURT. Q. When you discovered your loss, did you look about for the thieves? A. Yes; we looked down the different streets, and searched—Richards was found in a public-house that night—I was told he had got away from the officer—he was found at his mother's, two or three days afterwards, in a chimney—he sent me word on Monday morning that he had done it; and when we found him in the chimney, he asked me to forgive him, and said he would make it all right to me, and his mother should go and get the money—I did not tell him it would be better for him to confess, nor did the policeman.
JURY. Q. What was your answer to him when he said he would make it all right? A. I said the policeman was below, and he must go with him.
MATTHEW BROMLEY . I am a waterman. I was in the public-house that evening, and saw Richards come in at the street-door.
ELIZABETH CARTER . I am the wife of William Carter—we keep a public-house at Deptford. Richards came to my house with a young man, for a pint of porter—I sent it into the tap-room—they were there about a quarter of an hour—Dilliman then came in—they went away in about twenty minutes.
THOMAS ROSE ( police-constable R 112.) At half-past nine o'clock in the evening of the 22nd of March, I went and found Richards at the Blue Bell, Butcher-row—I found on him twelve penny-pieces, and twenty-five halfpence—I took him to the prosecutor's, aad he made his escape by a side door—I then took Ogden at his own house—I found on him six penny-pieces and eleven halfpence—about two o'clock on Monday morning I found this till, with some papers, some old locks, and other things in it, thrown over the garden of the Bee-hive public-house in Flagon-row.
WILLIAM SMITH ( police-constable R 38.) I took Richards into custody—I found him secreted up the chimney in his father's bed-chamber—he showed great contrition, and wished I would send for Diiliman and Ogden, that taey might make the money up to Mr. Francis—I said he must go with me, and on the way to the station-house, be confessed that he was in the Duke of Sussex public-house, in New-street, about eight o'clock—that Diiliman came in, and asked him to go With him to the water-gates, to lay a barge on shore, bet on passing down New-street to the lower water-gates, Diiliman asked him to go and take the till—that he went in and took it, and they ran on to Hughes' Fields, where they shared the money, and then threw the till away.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean that you screwed this confession out of him? A. Upon my oath I did not—I did not tell him it would be better for him to tell me—he told me this voluntarily—when he told me this, neither of the other prisoners were present.
JAMES THOMAS FRANCIS . I am a waterman. I was coming down Hughes' Fields, and saw Richards with something like a box under his arm.
CHARLES EDWARDS ( police-constable R 15.) About twelve o'clock that night, I went to Dilliman's—I knocked at the door, and his father
opened it—I took Dilliman—he said he had not been with Richards since four o'clock that afternoon—the till was afterwards found.
(Harriet Webster gave Richards a good character.)
RICHARDS— GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.
DILLIMAN and OGDEN— NOT GUILTY .
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Recorder.

1098. JOHN McKILLICK was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March, 1 gown, value 6 d.; 1 frock, value 3 d.; 1 flannel petticoat, value 9 d.; and 2 aprons, value 2 d.; the goods of Sarah Robinson.
SARAH ROBINSON . I am a widow, and live at Greenwich. On the 26th of March, I hung some linen to dry in front of the house; among which was a gown, a frock, and two aprons—I missed them the same evening.
JAMES WILD ( police-constable R 141.) On the 26th of March, I went to East-lane, Greenwich, and saw the prisoner at Mr. Smith's. He escaped from there, by getting over a wall—I went immediately to his lodging, and waited in the passage till he came—I then told him I took him for that wet linen out of Hogg-lane—he said, "Say nothing about it, and I will give you all I have about me"—I said, that was not the way I did my buisness—I went into his room, and found these articles in a box—he wanted me to put them back.
Prisoner. This wall is twelve feet high, and has glass bottles on the top—I could not get over it—I did not rent the room he went to. Witness. He showed me the room, and said it was his box—he said he took the linen for a lark, and did know it belonged to this poor woman.
Prisoner. I expected it was Sarah Nichols's.
JOHN SAVIOUR . I am a labourer, and lodge in East-lane. On the night of the 26th of March, I was at Berry's house, in Hogg-lane—the prisoner came in there, and said he was going to the other side of the College—after he had been there a little while, he trod on my toes, to make me go out with him—he said there were some handkerchiefs hanging in a court, and he went and took them down—he offered me some, but I would not have them—he then told me to follow him, which I did—he went home, and left them there—he wanted me to sell them, but I would not—we then came down together—he went one way, and I went and told the policeman—I have had no dispute with him—he said, while he was unpinning the things, that he took them for a lark.
Prisoner. I went to Mr. Berry's, to inquire if he had got the piece of baize. Witness. That was the night before—two boys told me that you had made inquiry about an old rug—I had sold it for old rags, and it fetched 2 d.—when you took these things, you said, "Never mind about the piece of rug"—we had no quarrel about it.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
NOT GUILTY .
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1099. CHARLES STEVENS was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. SHEA conducted the Prosecution.
FREDERICK ROUCH . I keep a cheesemonger's shop, on Deptford-bridge, at Greenwich. On the 11th of September, about seven o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came into my shop for a quarter of a pound of Cheshire cheese—I served him—it came to 3 d., or 3 1/2 d.—he said it was rather
more than he wanted—I told him it would not spoil, and he could put it into his pocket—he gave me half-a-crown—I put it on the ledge, not in the till—I gave him the change—he went out, and in about five minutes he returned, for some more cheese—I said, "I thought I cut you too much before"—he said, "A friend of mine has got that, and I want a quarter of a pound more for myself"—I served him, and he threw down another half-crown—I said, "It is a bad one"—he said, "No such thing"—I said, "Look at it, it is bad"—it then struck me that the other half-crown was bad—I looked at it, and it was, bad—I said to him, "You gave me a bad half-crown five minutes ago"—he said he had not been in the shop before—I went round to him, and said, "I am awkwardly situated, having no shopman here; if you will give me up the change, I will let you go"—he said he had not been there before—I called in a policeman, and gave him in charge.
WILLIAM DYKE . I was called in, and took the prisoner—I received these two half-crowns from the prosecutor.
JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin to the Mint. These half-crown pieces are both counterfeit, and cast in the same mould.
Prisoner's Defence. I leave it to yourselves to say whether it stands to reason that I should go to his shop and offer two half-crowns within five minutes.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined One Year.

1102. HENRY THOMPSON, alias Preston , was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of February, at St. Mary, Lambeth, Surrey, 1 waistcoat, value 6 s., the goods of William Hickinbottom; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM CLARKE . I am in the employ of William Hickinbottom, a pawnbroker, in New-cut, Lambeth. On the 21st of February the prisoner was brought in with this waistcoat, which had been inside the shop—it if my master's.
EDWARD LANGLEY . I am a policeman. On the 21st of February I saw the prisoner, in company with another, pawing the prosecutor's shop—I had my eye on them for some time—I saw the prisoner reach his arm inside
the door, snatch down the waistcoat, and walk away with it under his arm—the other remained standing in front of the shop—I secured the prisoner with the waistcoat under his arm.
