[trialAccount t18350406-1102a]
1102. THOMAS WOODCOCK was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Cutler, about the hour of three in the night of the 12th of March, at Kew, Surrey, with intent to steal, and feloniously and burglariously stealing therein 3 coats, value 2 l. 10 s.; 1 waistcoat, value 16 s.; 12 yards of linen cloth, value 15 s.; 1 shirt, value 6 d.; 3 handkerchiefs, value 1 s.; and 6 sovereigns; the goods and monies of Benjamin Cockayne.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the dwelling-house of Benjamin Cockayne.
BENJAMIN COCKAYNE . I am servant to Mr. Cutler, who lives at Kew-green. I saw all my clothes safe about a quarter past six o'clock in the evening of the 12th of March; there were three coats, one waistcoat, twelve yards of linen cloth, one shirt, three handkerchiefs, one pair of gloves, and six sovereigns—I saw them in my box, which was locked, but the key was in it—the box was in my room, over master's stable—I went away at a quarter after six o'clock, and returned at a quarter before seven o'clock—when I left, the outer door of the stable was shut, but not locked—the yard-door was locked—the stable is all enclosed in the same fence as the dwelling-house—it has no communication with the dwelling-house—it is some distance from the house—I saw the prisoner at a quarter past six o'clock that evening—he stood at the stable yard gate, and asked me for work—I told him I had no work for him, but that I would try to get him some—I had known him about a week or ten days—he had a bag in his hand at the time—I could not swear to the bag again—he staid there not more than two minutes, and then went to his master—I went out with my master in his phaeton, and returned about a quarter before seven o'clock—as I was taking the horse from the phaeton, to put it into the stable, the prisoner came to me—he did not assist me—he called as he was going home, and said he had not got any work from his master, but he was to go again to-morrow morning, and he would give him some—he bid me good night—I bid him good night, locked up the yard, and went into my master's house—I found a linen-draper in the house—I bought three silk handkerchiefs of him—I was to give him 13 s. for them—I had occasion to go into the stable for the money, and found my money was all gone, and my clothes—I suspected the prisoner directly, and instantly got on a horse and rode to Richmond after him, and from there to Mortlake—I overtook him—I got to his house with the beadle before he did—I saw him about twenty yards from the door, and the instant he saw the beadle in his garden he ran off—I mounted my horse, and rode off to East Sheen, and gave information to the police—some time afterwards the policeman came, and said they had caught him—I went and found him in a shed, all in a perspiration—I saw him searched, and found one coat on his back, three handkerchiefs in his hat, and 3 s. in his pocket—I lost some silver—I cannot say how much.
Sixth Session, 1835. 1069
Cross-examined by MR. MAHON. Q. At what hour did you see him in custody? A. At eleven o'clock—he had the coat on his back—the handkerchiefs were common cotton ones—they had no mark on them—I did not know where he lived—his master told me—I saw him first at a quarter after six o'clock—it was light enough to see his face at that time—the six sovereigns are my own—I am a servant of all work—I had saved the six sovereigns—I was not out more than half an hour with my master—I drove him to a gentleman's house, a very short distance—I looked at my watch when I came back to see what time it was, and it was a quarter to seven o'clock—I saw my money when I went to my box at a quarter past six o'clock—it was loose in the bottom of my box, under my clothes—the coats are two body and a top coat—I have not found the sovereigns.
JOHN PAYNE . I am a sergeant of the police at Barnes. I was examined before the Magistrate—on the evening of the 12th of March, about a quarter before nine o'clock, I received information from the prosecutor that he had been robbed—he mentioned the prisoner's name—I went with Gomm, the policeman, into Mortlake-meadows; and in a shed in one of the meadows, I found the prisoner in a very great perspiration and agitation, as if just come in—he was almost breathless—I took him into custody, and took him to the station-house at Wandsworth—when I got him there, I took off his back a coat which I now produce—I took one handkerchief from his hat in the shed, and secured his hands at that time—these two handkerchiefs I took from his hat at the station-house—I took from his trowsers pocket 3 s., and this rasp was in the lining of the coat he had on, under the coat which I took off.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know the prisoner before? A. I never saw him before—the meadows were no great distance from his house, but he passed his house to get there—it was dark when we found him—we told him we were policemen—he was agitated before I spoke to him—I turned my light into the shed the moment I entered, and his face was all in a very great perspiration—I found no sovereigns on him—I searched him very closely—shoemakers use rasps—this was about nine o'clock, as near as possible—I saw the prosecutor about a quarter past nine o'clock—it was about eleven o'clock when we got to the station-house.
JOSEPH GOMM . I am a policeman at Wandsworth. I went with sergeant Payne, in search of the prisoner, into the meadows at Mortlake—I had seen the prosecutor before that, and he had informed us of the robbery—we searched through the meadow, and found the prisoner in a shed—Payne went in at one end of the shed, and I at the other—Payne turned on his light, and under the prisoner's feet I saw this coat—I picked it up, and asked the prisoner if it belonged to him—he said it did—I took Payne's lantern, and searched round the shed—in one corner I found this bag, in the hay-crib; and in it was one piece of cloth, one waistcoat, one shirt, one pair of gloves, and one coat, which the Magistrate allowed the prosecutor to take away—I kept the other property in my possession till I got to Wandsworth—I then searched every thing in the bag, to see if there was any money in the coat pockets; and then put every thing into the bag again, tied it up, and gave it to the inspector, Bignall, who locked it up in his cupboard for the night—in the morning he gave me the same things out of the cupboard, I can swear—I saw him unlock it about tea o'clock—I opened the bundle when I took it before the Magistrate, about eleven
o'clock, and I am sure they are the things I delivered to Bignall overnight—they were shown to the prosecutor that night at the station-house.
Cross-examined. Q. The shed was an open shed? A. Yes; any one might have gone into it—it was completely dusk—we came suddenly with the lantern upon him—I cannot say whether he had any opportunity of concealing any thing—I observed him as closely as I could—I found no sovereigns—I know the meadows well—there are no footpaths across them—the bag was in a corner of the shed, about seven yards from him—he did not claim the bag—I did not know him before—I did not know he lived at Mortlake—I have received information that he did—I found no money in the coat or bag—this is the coat I found under his feet.
JOHN PAYNE re-examined. Here is the coat I found on his back.
BENJAMIN COCKAYNE re-examined. These coats are mine—I wore the one that the prisoner had on, two or three days every week—I wear the other very often—I have had it about nine months—this piece of cloth I swear to—I have had it about three weeks—I have had the shirt some time—I have others of the same sort—I have no particular mark on it, nor on the gloves—I lost such gloves—the coat I have on was in the bag—I know it to be mine—I have worn it a good deal—I have had it about six months.
Prisoner. At the Magistrate's house the prosecutor said if I would give him 6 l. he would not appear against me.
GUILTY of stealing only. Aged 25.— Transported for Seven Years.
First Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18350406-1103]
1103. JOHN DYER was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, at St. George the Martyr, Southwark, Surrey, 1 watch, value 2 l.; 1 watch-ribbon, value 1 d.; and 2 keys, value 2 d.; the goods of Thomas Brownnut; and 2 spoons, value 10 s.; 2 sovereigns; 5 half-sovereigns; 2 crowns, and 10 half-crowns; the goods and monies of Esther Barton, in her dwelling-house.
ESTHER BARTON . I live at No. 7, Little Suffolk-street, Borough. I had a box in which I keep my clothes and money—it was under my bed—the prisoner lodged up stairs in another room between five and six months—I counted my money up for my landlord on the Monday—there was 6 l. 5 s. in money—a silver watch, worth 2 l., a table-spoon, and tea-spoon, worth 10 s.—my son had given me the watch and spoons to mind for him—a constable came to our door afterwards, and in consequence of that I searched my box, and my money and watch were gone—that was on Friday, the 27th—the prisoner had stopped out all night on Thursday.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Are you married? A. I am a widow—I have four rooms in my house, and four or five persons lodge there—one young man is a painter, another a young man who goes in and out of the Bench with messages—I always locked my door when I went out—other persons had an opportunity of going in and out of my bed-room as well as the prisoner—I had not seen the property after the Monday—I have not found the watch or spoons—the prisoner is a painter, and has been out of work a long time—he owed me nearly 2 l. for washing and lodging—I do not take any nightly lodgers, without they come from the country, and that has not been for a month—I have had the watch for years—it is a very good one—it is silver—I saw the prisoner last on Thursday morning.
Sixth Session, 1835. 1071
WILLIAM CORNISH . I am a policeman. I was at St. George's church about seven o'clock on Friday morning, the 27th, and the prisoner gave a woman in charge for robbing him—he was drunk—I said I must search him—I found 4 d. in his waistcoat pocket—he said he had got no more money—on searching him further, I found in his trowsers pocket a bag containing two sovereigns and five half-sovereigns—I asked if it was his own—he said it was, and he had worked for it—he was locked up for being tipsy—he gave his name and address, No. 7, Little Suffolk-street, Borough—I went there and saw the prosecutrix—she immediately went to her box—I returned to the station-house, and found on the prisoner a key which fitted the prosecutrix's box—it appeared as if it had been filed and altered to fit the box—he was asked by the Alderman where he got the money—he said he had got it from his uncle, on the Tuesday.
Cross-examined. Q. For what purpose were you called? A. The prisoner told me to take a woman into custody—he charged her with having stolen 2 l. 10 s. from his pocket—I found 4 l. 10 s. on him—I searched the girl, and searched him directly—it might take a quarter of an hour to go from the Borough to our station-house—the prisoner walked behind me to the station-house, and the girl with me—this is a common key—the prosecutrix produced a key, and unlocked the box in my presence—I have both keys here—the woman's key was quite plain, a taper key, and the other is filed—the box is here.
COURT. Q. How far off the prosecutrix's house did you find the prisoner? A. About a mile.
MR. PAYNE. Q. He followed you to give charge of the girl? A. Yes—he could have run away if he liked—I found no watch or spoon—he did not tell me the money was sent up to him from the country—he said, that he had it from his uncle, on Tuesday, before Sir John Key, and I was directed to go to his uncle—I did not hear him say that; I was at the Town-hall—there were two Aldermen, and he made quite a different statement at the time he was committed.
ESTHER BARTON re-examined. He owed me nearly 2 l., for lodging—I never asked him for it, as he has been out of employ nearly four months—I knew he was very poor, and I thought he would pay me in the summer.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not know that he had friends in the country? A No—I never saw that key laying about—I know my own key—it is not filed.
Prisoner's Defence. I am very sorry to be placed at this bar for such a case—I have certainly been lodging with the prosecutrix some time, and she has stated to me over and over again, that she was in real distress, and could scarcely make both ends meet—I expected daily and hourly to be turned out of my lodging—I have had a few pounds from my friends, as I sent to them for a little assistance—it was mostly with the intent to pay the prosecutrix, as she pleaded poverty, and being very hardly done by her lodgers, who had been hardly put to it—she behaved as a mother to me—I sent into the country for 5 l., which I had from a friend of mine to pay her, and recruit myself with a few clothes, and get my tools ready to go to work again—I know very well she has been receiving money from the poor-house, which made me believe her very poor—as to the box, I never knew any thing of it—I never saw it till I was apprehended—as to the key, in my business it is a very useful thing, to use to graze off the glass when the fingers will not touch it—I went to Sadler's Wells on the evening
previous—I fell in with a friend or two, and spent a few shillings—I went and had something to drink—on my way home I laid down, having had too much—I went to sleep—I awoke about five in the morning, and fell in with a young girl in Great Suffolk-street—we had a cup of coffee together, and went from there, near St. George's Church—I there found I was robbed—I turned my pockets inside out before the landlord, who said, "Give her into custody"—I did so, and 3 s. were found upon her—when I got to the station-house, whether the policeman put it into my pocket, I cannot swear; but I am positive when I turned my pockets out, I had only fourpence.
MRS. BARTON re-examined. I lost two sovereigns, and five half-sovereigns; two dollars, and the rest in half-crowns—it was in a piece of rag.
Cross-examined. Q. If you had been asked before the policeman told you what he had found, could you have told of what your money consisted? A. Yes, I could—I cannot say how many half-crowns there were.
GUILTY of stealing to the value of 4 l. 10 s. only. Aged 27.— Transported for Seven Years.
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Justice Gaselee.
[trialAccount t18350406-1104]
1104. ROBERT SHERWIN and THOMAS CONSTABLE were indicted for stealing, on the 25th of March, 7 fowls, value 14 s., the property of Robert Alexander Gray, and that they had both been before convicted of felony.
HENRY DUNSTER . I am servant to Robert Alexander Gray, who lives at Camberwell. On the 25th of March, I fed his fowls between four and five o'clock in the afternoon—I fastened the door afterwards—I went to the hen-house the next morning with the policeman, between seven and eight o'clock, and found the hen-house door broken open—I missed seven hens and a cock—I had seen them the day before, between four and five o'clock—I saw them again at the station-house at Caraberwell, and knew them again—they were then dead.
JAMES MANN . I am a policeman—I know the prisoners—I saw them at six o'clock on Thursday morning, the 25th of March, on the high road between Camberwell and Peckham, within two or three hundred yards of Mr. Gray's house—I went a few yards up my beat, and missed them out of the road—I went to the next policeman and spoke to him—I borrowed his light, returned, and went on Mr. Pope's premises, adjoining Mr. Gray's—I got upon the fence, and saw the two prisoners coming from Mr. Gray's hen-house—I had the light full on their faces—I saw Sherwin with some fowls in each hand—I called to them to stop, but they ran away from me—Sherwin dropped the fowls as he ran—I sprang my rattle, and gave information of what had happened—I picked up the fowls and took them to the station-house—I went back to Mr. Gray's and examined the heu-house—I found the door broken open, and the bolt of the lock lying by the side of it—I went at seven o'clock in the morning to the residence of Sherwin, I opened the door, went into the passage, and saw his father there—I told him I wanted his son Robert—his father fetched him down stairs—I took him to the station-house at Camberwell—when I got him there, I told him what I took him for—he denied all knowledge of the robbery—I knew both prisoners for ten or twelve years—I am confident they are the men.
Cross-examined. Q. How long before that night had you seen them?
A. I do not think I have seen them for three months before—I am in the habit of seeing them two or three times a week sometimes, and sometimes not for months together—I will swear I had seen them within three months of the time in question—I saw them distinctly as they came under the lamp-post—I cannot say whether it was a dark night or not—I did not take particular notice—it was star-light—it was not moon-light—I saw their faces quite distinctly.
Q. Was it light enough to see them without the aid of any lamp at all? A. Not without being very close—I saw them by a gas-light, and the next time I had my comrade's lantern—I borrowed the lantern, because I was going into the back premises where there was no gas-light—they were on the other side of the road when I first saw them—the gas-light was on the same side as they were—I was meeting them—they were in my sight for about a minute then—I was standing still, and looked after them—they were a very short time in my sight when I had the lantern—they made their exit as soon as possible—Gray's house stands back from the road, nearly 100 yards—the hen-house is at the back of the house—I stood on the edge of the fence, looking over it—my feet were on the edge of the fence, nearly two feet from the ground—I had one hand on the rail, and held the light in their faces with the other.
Q. Before you went to Sherwin's house, did you call on a Mr. Skinner? A. No, he came to me about a quarter before seven o'clock—I believe he is a bricklayer, living at Peckham—I went to Sherwin's at seven o'clock—I now know that Sherwin's brother works with Skinner—I never heard him called him by the name of the tall sailor—I did not tell Skinner that I wanted the tall sailor—I had a conversation with Skinner about the tall sailor—I never told him that I wanted the tall sailor—he did not say "That cannot be, for he was at work for me"—Skinner came to me and asked if I wanted one of his men—I said, I did not know who his men were—he told me his name was Sherwin, and the man I wanted he suspected was Sherwin—I said, I had seen his man come out of Sherwin's house, and he was not the man I wanted—I afterwards told Skinner that I could not swear that it was Tom Constable that I saw that night—I did not think proper to satisfy Mr. Skinner, that he might not go and give him information to get out of the way.
JAMES WHITE . I am a policeman. I have a certificate of the former conviction of Constable ( read)—I was present at his trial in March, 1834.
JOHN COLLIER . I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of the former conviction of Sherwin by the name of Wilson, alias Sherwin ( read)—I was present at his trial—he is the man.
Sherwin's Defence. I went home about eleven o'clock that night, and did not get out of bed till the policeman called for me at eleven o'clock the next morning.
Constable's Defence. I went home about half-past ten o'clock, and went to bed—I got up at six o'clock next morning and went to work.
WILLIAM CONSTABLE . I live at Peckham. I am the father of the prisoner Constable—on the night of the 25th of March, I went to bed about half-past ten o'clock—my son slept in the same bed with me—he went to bed at the same time as I did—I fastened up the doors before I went to bed—my son came in just after I got to bed, and he fastened the doors up—I went to sleep about eleven or twelve o'clock—I awoke again and went to sleep afterwards—I awoke between five and six o'clock in the morning, and called my son up—he was in bed at that time—I should
think he could not have gone out in the night without my knowledge—it was impossible for him to get out without my knowledge, I am go wakeful—when I awoke in the night, he was in bed—he laid close against me—that was between eleven and twelve o'clock.
COURT. Q. What does your family consist of? A. Only one more, that is a girl—she sleeps in another room by herself.
CHARLES PERRENE . I am pot-boy at the Bull public-house, at Peckham. I know the prisoner Constable—he lives up the Bull-yard—hit father's house faces the Bull gates—I remember the night of the 25th of March—I saw Constable in the tap-room—he left between half-past ten and eleven o'clock—I may say a quarter to eleven o'clock—he unlaced his boots before he left, which was his usual practice—I went out with him and saw him go into his father's house—after he had got in, I distinctly heard him fasten three doors—I saw no more of him that night.
COURT. Q. Does he stay at your house every night till eleven o'clock? A. He generally stops there till nine or ten o'clock, not generally till eleven o'clock—I had occasion to go into the yard that night—he usually unlaces his boots at our house—the Bull is about half a mile from Mr. Gray's, or it may be three quarters of a mile.
JOHN SHERWIN . I am a labourer, and live at home with my father. On the night of the 25th of March I slept in my brother's room—he came home at eleven o'clock—I heard him go to bed directly after he came home—we both sleep in the same room—I did not go to sleep before three o'clock next morning—my brother was in bed from eleven till three o'clock—Hurley slept in the same room, and was there that night.
COURT. Q. What caused you to lay awake from eleven till three o'clock? A. I am troubled with boils—I was at work for Mr. Booker, a green-grocer, on the 25th of March—I leave there about three or four o'clock in the afternoon, and do nothing after that—my brother was taken next morning.
WILLIAM SHERWIN . I am the prisoner's father, I am a labouring man, and live at Peckham-Rye. I am afflicted with rheumatism, and cannot work now—the prisoner lives with me—on the 25th of March he came home about eleven o'clock at night—I let him in—I saw him go up stairs to go to bed—my wife gave him a light, and went up afterwards and fetched it down—I sleep in the room below his—I did not see him again till the policeman called at seven o'clock the next morning—I did not sleep from eleven o'clock that night till four o'clock the next morning—I am very much afflicted with rheumatism—my son never quitted my premises between his going to bed and the policeman coming in the morning—he was in bed and asleep when the policeman came—I awoke him myself—Hurley lives in my house—he slept in the same room as my son.
EDWARD HURLEY . I am a day-labourer, and lodge in Sherwin's house. On the 25th of March last I fell in company with the prisoner in the Borough, at half-past nine o'clock, and was with him until eleven o'clock that night—I was with him at the Nelson's Head public-house, at the entrance of the North-fields—I was in bed ten minutes or a quarter of an hour before he came to bed—I sleep in the same room with him—I can safely say he was in bed five or ten minutes after eleven o'clock—I was in bed before the clock struck eleven some time, myself—I arose about half-past eight o'clock—I might say I awoke seven or eight times during the night—I was not fatigued with work, as I was not busy at that time—every time that I awoke, I noticed the prisoner was asleep in the next bed
to me—I seldom awoke without heating him sleeping; or if he was awake and I was awake, I generally spoke to him—we spoke twice during the night—as near as I can guess, I spoke to him between one and two o'clock, or it might be twenty minutes after one o'clock—my watch was on the table, but I did not look at it—I spoke to him about twenty minutes after three o'clock—he was snoring, and did not answer me.
THOMAS SHERWIN . I live in the same house with my brother Robert. I remember his coming home about eleven o'clock, on the 25th of March—I did not Bee him go to bed—he went up stairs—I spoke to him as he went up—he slept in the room above me—the leg of the bedstead had fallen through the ceiling one night, and made a hole in it, and I could hear any thing—I must have heard him if he had gone out that night.
NOT GUILTY .
First Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18350406-1105]
1105. HUSTINGS MOORE and WILLIAM GROBECKER were indicted for a conspiracy.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT WYATT GRAINGE . I am a brickmaker, and live at Uxbridge. On the 25th of September, the prisoner Moore came to me at Uxbridge, and said he had been down to my field at Drayton, three miles from ux-bridge, and had seen some bricks there which he thought would suit him—he asked me if they were for sale—I said, yes, and I should be glad to sell them—I asked him if it was to be a ready-money transaction—he said, "No"—I said, as he was an entire stranger to me, I should not think of letting my goods go unless I had good security for them—he said if I would meet him in London, he would take me to his ground-landlord, who would be answerable for the amount of the bricks—ha appointed for me to meet him next day at eleven o'clock, at the Bell, Holborn, to go with him to the ground-landlord—I got there at twelve o'clock—Moore was not there—I left the Bell, and bad a message, which fetched me back—I returned to the Bell, and found Moore there—he had a man with him whom I found afterwards to be named Toovey—he called him his foreman—we agreed on the price of the bricks—I set off with Moore to go to the ground-landlord, in a cab—while riding, I asked him how far it was—he said not far, that it was at the Retreat, at South Lambeth—I asked him his ground-landlord's name—he said, Grobecker, and that he kept a large grammar-school—we at last got to the Retreat—it was a very large house, with a lawn in front, and a garden and two large hot-houaea behind—we got out there—the servant came to the door—I accompanied Moore through the house into the garden—we found Grobecker there—Moore introduced me, and said, "This is Mr. Grainge; I have been down to look at his bricks for the buildings in Sidney-square"—he had told me before that he was going to build Sidney-square, and a street there, and should want about 300,000 bricks—I said, "Mr. Moore has been looking at my bricks, and says you are the ground-landlord, and are going to build Sidney-square, and the street there—will you be the security for the bricks?"—Grobecker said, "Yes, I am—it makes no difference whether I pay you, or Moore—have you bargained for credit?"—I said I had no objection to give the usual trade credit—upon which Grobecker said, "Perhaps we shall manage it better than that, when we have got the goods?"
—I was perfectly satisfied with that, and returned into the City with Moore—he was to write to me when to send them, and in consequence of a letter afterwards, I delivered the bricks—I know Moore's writing—I delivered 290,000 and odd bricks—these are the delivery tickets, signed by Moore, as I sent them—the value is 546 l. odd—I delivered them to him on the faith of the representation of Grobecker, made in Moore's presence, of his being the ground-landlord, and no other.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Are you quite sure the representation made by Moore was, that Grobecker was the ground-landlord of the houses in Sidney-square? A. And a street near there; and I am quite sure that Grobecker said he was—I should not have let my bricks have gone unless he had—it was on Friday, the 25th or 26th of September, that I went to Grobecker's house with Moore—I sent a Bill of Exchange to Grobecker for his acceptance, after sending some bricks—that was the agreement—the bills were to be drawn by Moore, and accepted by Grobecker—I sent three Bills of Exchange to Grobecker for his acceptance—I have no recollection of sending to him for the acceptance of a bill which I had indorsed before I sent it—I recollect a bill coming back in consequence of Moore's writing—I have no recollection of sending him a bill with an indorsement.
Q. Was not the representation made, that Grobecker would be security for Moore; and as a counter security, he had got the agreement of his ground-landlord for houses in Mile-end-road? A. I never heard of an agreement till after Fagan told me I was dealing with swindlers—Mr. Catlin was not employed as my attorney to apply to these parties for payment of the Bills of Exchange—I have not seen an agreement between Moore and his ground-landlord, in the possession of Mr. Catlin—I saw it in the possession of the police-sergeant—I was told that Grobecker would be security for Moore for the payment of the bricks, and Grobecker agreed to it—he agreed to pay me, and said it made no difference whether he paid me or Moore.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. When did you employ Mr. Catlin as your attorney? A. After Inspector Fagan sent me two or three letters, saying I was dealing with swindlers, and begging of me to come to town directly—that was three months after Grobecker agreed to become security.
THOMAS CARTLEDGE . I am deputy secretary in the Lunatic Office. I am agent for three of the grantors of land in Green wood-street, Sidney-square—Grobecker has nothing to do with the land—here is the agreement.
Cross-examined. Q. Is there any dispute about the title to the premises? A. No; the agreement applies to Greenwood-street—it has nothing to do with Sidney-square—it is Greenwood-street, Mile-end-road—Moore has built some skeletons—I never saw them but once—I believe they are not roofed—there are some bricks on the premises—I know nothing of Sidney-square.
AUGUSTUS WOOLSON . I am solicitor for Mr. Hawkins, the freeholder, and ground-landlord of Sidney-square—Grobecker has no interest in it—I am solicitor for the freeholder, and also for the lessee of part of it, Mr. Wheeler.
Cross-examined. Q. Do not two parties claim the ground? A. There are two lessees—I know nothing of Mr. Pratt—Cohen is a tenant of Mr. Hawkins—there are no contending parties, nor any dispute about the title, to my knowledge—I do not know who let Moore the ground in the Square
to build the houses on—the assignees of Mr. Bedford hold part of it—that is Mr. Handley and another—I do not know Anthony Pratt, of Mile-end-road.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is Grobecker the ground-landlord? A. Certainly not the freeholder; nor a lessee under Hawkins.
FRANCIS FAGAN . I am an inspector of the police—I apprehended Moore on the night of the 17th of February, at half-past twelve o'clock, at the Britannia public-house, Hoxton-old-town—I asked him if his name was Hustings Moore—he said it was not—I repeatedly asked if it was Hustings Moore—he denied that it was—I said, "It matters not, you are the man I want, you must come with me"—I said I must take him into custody—he said he was a man of independent property, and had property at Knightsbridge, worth £15,000—on our way to the station, I asked him him how long he had known Grobecker—he said, "I have known him about five months"—he said, "This will complete my affair, and make my action all the stronger against this man Griange—I never performed a more mercantile affair in my life than this"—on the following morning I apprehended Grobecker, at the Retreat, South Lambeth—as I took him to the station, he asked me what would be the punishment for the offence alleged in the warrant—I told him I believed about a couple of years' imprisonment—I asked him if he was ground-landlord of Sidney-square, or the street near the Earl Grey—he said, "No; no more than you are"—I said, "What, have you no land?"—he said, "Not a bit"—I asked him how long he had known Moore—he said, "About eighteen months."
Cross-examined. Q. Moore denied his name being Hustings Moore? A. Yes—I had a private coat on, but he knew me very well to be a police-officer, and I knew him well—I asked him his name, because it was necessary, as his name was contained in the warrant—I read the warrant to him afterwards—he knew I had not a writ—it was on Sunday morning—I know Greenwood-street—there is such a place as Sidney-square, and there was so on the 26th of September—one part of it is built on—I was not there for some time after the 28th of September—not where the buildings are, but some part of it was built some years ago—I believe four car-cases are run up to the first story—there may be five—I have not carried away any bricks from there—I have seen some carried away.
WILLIAM LINES . I am a bricklayer. I have been working for Moore, at the buildings in Sidney-square—all he has done there—thirty-three of forty thousand of Mr. Griange's bricks have been used there—not exceeding that—I have sold some of the bricks, by Moore's order—I sold thirteen thousand to Mr. Gardener, at 1 l. 8 s. per thousand—I sold six thousand to Perry, for 1 l. 9 s. per thousand—Moore ordered me to deliver them, which I did—I know Grobecker's hand-writing—I have seen two notes from him, addressed to Moore, making application for money; one demand was for a sovereign, and the other sum was not specified—that was since Mr. Griange's bricks were delivered—Moore has shown me notes from Grobeeker and told me he had to meet him at different places—he sent me to sell one or two thousand bricks, in order to raise the money to meet Grobecker—I have seen Moore at Abbott's beer-shop, in Mile-end-road, and have heard him talk about meeting Griange, at Uxbridge—he said he bought a certain portion of bricks that a man, named Burton, introduced him to the field—he said he met Griange by appointment in Holborn, and a man named Toovey acted as a partner at the time—he said he put Griange into a cab—
went down to Grobecker's, and staid there until he brought him away from the neighbourhood; that he should not stop there and make any inquiry, a Grobecker did not stand very well in the neighbourhood.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. A master bricklayer—Gardener is a wharfinger, in the Mile-end-road—the bricks were landed on his wharf, and part of them were sold to him in payment of his account for the wharfage—I do not know when Moore took the ground in Sidney-square, to build the houses—he was about it a long time—I began the buildings at the latter end of November, or beginning of December.
MR. PAYNE addressed the Court and Jury on behalf of Moore.
Grobecker's Defence. I did not acknowledge myself in any way to be the ground-landlord—the word, "ground-landlord," was not mentioned at the time of my interview with the prosecutor, and Sidney-square could not have been mentioned—it was not in contemplation to build houses there then—Moore had no notion of building houses there till after the commencement of November—the agreement for the land there is dated 17th of November, and the building did not take place till the end of November, or the beginning of December—I could not be a party to any representation—I can prove that the word, "ground-landlord," was not used at the conversation—I cannot say I have many witnesses, as I did not anticipate that out of so straightforward a transaction any charge of a criminal nature could arise—I might imagine I could not pay the bills, and be arrested, but could not imagine a proceeding of a criminal nature would be brought against me, and therefore I have not evidence as to all the conversation which took place at the interview; however, I have evidence of what did take place, and though it may be received with suspicion, still I will call that evidence.
WILLIAM GROBECKER . Jun. I remember Mr. Griange and Mr. Moore coming to my father, in September last. As near as I can recollect, it was about the 26th—I was in the dining parlour, on the ground floor, near the window, which is three or four feet from the ground—the window was open—I heard part of the conversation which took place—I heard a knock at the door—the servant went to the door—I immediately afterwards heard somebody passing through the passage—I saw my father approach the door—Moore and Griange went out of the back door, and met my father under the window—Moore introduced Mr. Griange, who said, "Mr. Grobecker, my business is very short with you; Mr. Moore has seen bricks in my field, which he seems to think will suit him to perform a building, which he has contracted to do"—he then said, "Mr. Grobecker, have you any objection to become security for these bricks?"—my father said, "No; I consider myself quite secure"—he then inquired what credit was to be given—Mr. Griange said, "Two months"—my father said, "That is short; cannot you make it three,—or I think it will be better to make it three, and then all will be right?"—Mr. Griange said, "We will see about that, Mr. Grobecker," and they then left.
COURT. Q. What are you? A. Clerk to an estate-agent.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you been living at home with your father long? A. Yes; about seven months—since that I have been backwards and forwards from home—I was living in his house in 1829—I was absent a few months, about seven months ago—I have heard the name of Luttrell—there was an old gentleman, named Luttrell, used to come backwards and forwards to the house—there was some circumstance between my father and him, I do not know what.
Q. Do you mean to say, on your oath, you do not know that your father
pleaded guilty, in 1829, to an indictment for defrauding that gentle-man of 300 l. in the King's Bench? A. I know there was an affair in the King's Bench—I know he was indicted, and pleaded guilty—I am twenty-one years old.
MOORE — GUILTY .—Aged 54.
GROBECKER— GUILTY .—Aged 51.
Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18350406-1106]
1106. WILLIAM BRADBURY was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of April, at St, George the Martyr, 1 half-crown, the money of Henry Chester, his master; and GEORGE TYLER , for that he, before the said felony was done and committed, to wit, on the same day and at the same parish, did feloniously and maliciously incite, move, procure, counsel, hire, and command the said William Bradbury to do and commit the said felony.
Bradbury pleaded GUILTY .— Confined Eight Days.
HENRY CHESTER . I am a bread and biscuit baker, and live in St. George's-circus, Southwark. Bradbury had been about six weeks in my service—on Friday, the 3rd of April, I was absent from home, and, on returning in the evening, Mrs. Cheater gave me information.
ELIZA CHESTER . I am the prosecutor's wife, Bradbury came to me for money to fetch 1 cwt. of salt—I gave him two half-crowns—he said we wanted salt—when he came back he brought me 1 s. 6 d.—when Mr. Chester came home I told him what I had sent for—Tyler has lived with us for about a year and a quarter, and was in our service at the time—he was a trusty servant, as we thought—I thought the salt was wanted for the baking—I supposed Bradbury had bought 1 cwt. of salt, as he gave me 1 s. 6 d. change—Tyler was in the bakehouse at the time—I do not know whether he could hear what passed.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. What is the price of salt? A. Three shillings and sixpence per cwt.—I have sent for salt before—we trusted to Tyler as to whether salt was wanted—Mr. Chester attends to the shop—this was on Saturday—Tyler's wages would be due on the Wednesday—no wages are due to him now—he was paid on the Saturday before he left the house—I did not hear any dispute between him and my husband about wages.
WILLIAM MOORE . I live in the London-road. I sold Bradbury half a cwt. of salt, for which he paid me 1 s. 9 d.—he gave me half-a-crown, and I gave him 9 d. change.
HENRY CHESTER re-examined. My wife told me about this salt, and, on the Saturday morning, I went into the bakehouse—both the prisoners were at work—I saw the salt-tub—some of it was spilled—I told Tyler he should make the boy more careful, and not allow him to spill salt over the tub—I said, "What have you done with the remainder of the hundredweight of salt?"—he said it was in the sack, put by—I spoke to Bradbury to bring it to me, to show it to me—they hesitated at doing that—I then went over to Moore's with Bradbury, and found he had only had half a hundredweight—I returned and spoke to Tyler—I took Bradbury into a back room by himself, and questioned him—he began to cry, and said, George, the man, had told him to do it; and that a young man, who lived with me some time before, used to do the same—I called Tyler out of the bakehouse, and questioned him before the policeman—I asked him if he knew of Bradbury fetching the salt—he said he did know of it, and of Joe doing it, who lived with me before, and he thought it was not his business to acquaint me of it—I asked him if he had any of the money—he said he agreed to drink some of the
beer, which was bought with the money, and which he told the boy to fetch—Bradbury said Tyler persuaded him to do it; and Tyler said "I knew he did it, for Joe used to do it, who lived here before."
WILLIAM MITCHELL . I am a policeman. Last Saturday week the prosecutor fetched me to his house—Bradbury was called from the parlour and Tyler from the bakehouse—the prosecutor said, "George, I am going to give you into custody about the salt"—Tyler said, "Very well, you may do as you please; it would not have happened had you behaved to me as you ought to have done"—the prosecutor said, "In what way have I believed haved to you?"—Tyler said, "I shall not state that here"—the prosecutor said, "You ought to know better"—he said, "Why, the other boy used to do it—I told Bradbury to get the salt, and I certainly had some of the beer and tobacco which was bought with the money"—on the way to the station-house, I said, "Tyler, you are the father of children, you ought to have known better than to persuade a boy to do this kind of act"—he said, "I should not have done it if Mr. Chester had behaved to me as he ought to have done."
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean to swear he said he had part of the money? A. Part of the beer and tobacco which the money produced.
JOSEPH CANT ( police-constable L 174.) I was on duty on Monday, the 4th, in St. George's-circus, and was called in with my brother officer—Mr. Chester told me he suspected he had been robbed—I heard Tyler say he knew the prosecutor had been robbed before, by a servant who previously lived with him—Chester said, "Why should you allow such a thing? I always pay you well"—he replied, that Mr. Chester had used him ill lately, for some reasons, but he should not tell him what, then.
Tyler's Defence. I did drink out of the beer that the boy fetched, and I knew he did it—after he did it, he told me he had done it—I gave him money to buy the tobacco myself.
(William Slacker, baker, Charlton-crescent, Islington; William Whittock, Hornsey-row, Islington; and Joseph Andrews, painter and glazier, Waterloo-road, gave the prisoner a good character.)
TYLER — GUILTY. Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18350406-1107]
1107. ABRAHAM ABRAHAMS was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. RYLAND and JONES conducted the Prosecution.
MARY ANN SAVAGE . I am a widow. In March, last year, I kept a small shop in Free-school-street—a man came into my shop to buy some peppermint-drops—I did not know him at that time—he was dressed as a livery servant—I have seen him since—his name is Barnet Magnus—while he was in the shop, the prisoner came to the door, and offered Magnus 7 l. for some coats—when Magnus came in, he asked for an ounce of peppermint-drops which I served him with—he began to say his master had died, and he had brought these coats to dispose of for the butler—he had them tied up in a bundle—I had not seen them—he told me they were in the bundle, and asked me to allow him to leave them in the parlour—I said, "Yet, you can leave them, but it is strange"—he said, "My good lady, I have met a Jew in the Kent-road, and I really cannot get rid of him," and if I would be so kind as to allow him, he would take them into the parlour; and with that, he walked out of the shop into the parlour—I had not seen the prisoner at that time—Magnus went to the window, which is acrow the room, and began taking out the clothes, and showing them to me—I then
saw that they were coats—he then asked me if I would buy those coats—I told him, no, that I could sell him coats—he said, "My good lady, you can just look at them; I just want you to look at them; that is all"—I said, "Yes, I see them, they are all very well"—he tied them up again—I turned round, and Abrahams was inside the parlour, at the door—I had not seen him before—(I have not the slightest doubt of his being the man—I afterwards saw him in Newgate, and was requested to point out the man—I stood on a form, and was told to stand there till I could see him, and I saw him corat out of a door, and I said, "That is the man"—I am sure he is the man)—while Magnus was showing me the coats, he said they were worth 15 guineas; that was before the prisoner came in; and he said it afterward too—he said the Jew had offered him 7 l. 10 s. for them—when I saw the prisoner at the room-door, I said to him, "My good man, what do you want here?"—he said, "My good lady, I hope no offence, I have come to know if the man will take the money for these clothes"—I said, "My good man, what do you want here? I know nothing of you; I wish you would leave my place"—he said, "My good lady, excuse me, but it is a good offer; the coats are worth fifteen guineas"—he said they would fetch him 12 l. in a sale, and that he kept a shop at the top of the Kent-road—I said, "Well, I know nothing about it"—he said, "My good lady, I have a wife and ten children; I am a respectable man, and keep a respectable shop"—Magnus had gone out at this time—the prisoner asked if I would buy the coats—I said no, I would not—he said, if I would not buy the coats, would I pay the sum of 7 l., and he would leave me a deposit, that he should return the money in less than half an hour—I said I would have nothing to do with it, for I did not know that the coats were not stolen; and then in came Magnus back again—the prisoner wished then to know if I was going to pay this money—I said so, I would have nothing to do with it—he said he knew that the coats could not be stolen—that he knew where the livery servant lived—that he knew Magnus as a livery servant in the Kent-road—he did not call him Magnus, he said he knew that man to be a livery servant, and to live in the Kent-road—there was no other man there at the time—he said he knew that his master had died—he pressed me to advance the money, finding I would not buy them—I then took four sovereigns out of my pocket, and laid them on the table—I took them up again and put them into my pocket, and said, "No, I will have nothing to do with you; go out of my place"—I then went to go to the door to call Susan, who was then living with me, to get rid of these men—Abrahams caught hold of one of my bands, and violently held me, and pushed me by the stomach as I was calling Susan—I said, "You will please to unloose me, and not hold me," and then he did unloose me, but he would not let me go into my shop—I felt frightened, and thought, somehow or other, if I did not give the money, it would be forcibly taken from me; they haviig asked me before that, if I had got 7 l.—I asked, why they should ask me that.
Q. How did he prevent you? going out of the room? A. He laid hold of me, and pressed me violently—I was very ill at the time—the other man stood at the window—I was frightened, being alone—I then laid the seven sovereigns down—Susan did not come near me—Abrahams said, "We want no Susan here, my good lady, come, make haste and give me the money; make haste that I may be gone"—Magnus came and took the sovereigns up off the table—Abrahams was standing next the door—he never moved from the door—he could not shut it, as there was a great mat
—he had a clothes-bag on his shoulder—he was near enough to see Magnus take up the sovereigns—he said, "Pay him, my good lady, pay the man; I will give you a deposit, and will bring you the money in less than half an hour; you are doing a deed of charity"—after Magnus took up the sovereigns, he took the coats up, and laid them on a chair—he said, "Shall I leave them in this handkerchief?"—I said, "I do not know; if you know the man"—he said, "Oh, I will take the handkerchief," Abrahams gave me 5 s., and they both went off together, leaving the coats on the chair—neither of them ever returned—they never came to my house again—about a month afterwards, I was walking out; a man, dressed not as a livery servant, came up, and asked me how I did—I turned round, and said, "Why you are the man that had my money"—it was Magnus—he said, "No, my good lady, you are quite mistaken; I keep a respectable tin and brazier's shop in Petticoat-lane"—he showed me a card with a name on it—I said, "I do not want the card; I want my money"—he said, "Will you come and have something to drink?"—I said, "No, you had my money, that is what I want"—I looked for a policeman, but could not see one, and he made his escape—I was walking down the Borough three or four months afterwards with my sister; I had got a little before her; I turned round, and saw Magnus speaking to my sister—I stopped, and asked her what he was saying to her—she said he was asking who I was, what I was, and where I lived—I told her to have nothing to do with him, for he was the man who had my money—he said, "Woman, you have accused me of that before; I am not the man"—he said, "Will you not come and have some drink?" I said, "No, it is my money, and not drink I want"—I could not get a policeman, and he went away—about six or seven weeks ago, I met him again in Fenchurch-street, while the prisoner was in Newgate for another offence, I was walking with my brother; I said, "Here is that Barnet Magnus"—on which he said, "How do you do?"—I said, "Never mind how I do, I am very ill, but I will do you; Mr. Abrahams is now in Newgate, and I shall intercede about the money you had of mine"—he called me a fool and an ass; said I was drunk, and a mad woman—he abused me very much—I was not able to give him in charge—I was ill, and was led in the street by my brother—I could not walk alone—it was on a Sunday—he said, "This is the third time you have charged me with this"—I said, "You are the man"—he was not dressed as a livery servant, but I was sure he was the man—I was not able to give him in charge, and he got away—I saw Abrahams after that—I had met him before that on Holborn-hill, and given him in custody—I am certain he is the man.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long ago is it that you gave him in custody? A. I really cannot say—it was in the summer of last year; I had him taken before a Magistrate—he was let at liberty again—I did not swear positively that he was the man—I was so exhausted in running after him that I could not—it took away my speech—I did not sit down and rest myself—it might be eleven or twelve o'clock when I took him before a Magistrate—the Magistrate did not tell me to sit down and collect myself—they said unless I could state it better they could do nothing in it—the prisoner's wife has been to me three different times, and offered 3 l. one time—when I went they offered me 2 l.—I said I would not have any thing to do with it—I had a baby in my arms six or eight weeks old—I did not examine the coats—I lost my husband in August, 1833.
MR. RYLAND. Q. You say the day you saw him you ran a good way
after him, and was exhausted? A. Yes—the Magistrate said no doubt they should soon have him again—I afterwards saw in the paper that he was in custody, and went and saw him.
GEORGE EDWARDS . I am the brother of Mrs. Savage. I was with her about six weeks ago, going down Fenchurch-street; all on a sudden she looked up, and said, "Here is Barney Magnus"—he said, "How do you do, Ma'am?"—she said, "I will do you now; I have got Abrahams, and now I will have you"—he called her a fool, and every thing else.
SUSAN JENKINS . In March, last year, I lived with the prosecutrix—I remember two men coming into her shop—the prisoner was one of them—a man came in, in the character of a footman, and asked for an ounce of peppermint-drops—while he was there, the prisoner came in—I took particular notice of him.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you in the shop all the while? A. Yes; I did not see the man take away the money—they were in the back room—they went in and out two or three times—I have not seen the prisoner since, but I can swear he is the man—the reason I noticed him was, I wondered why he came in and out so—I noticed he was a dark man—he came in and out for about half an hour—I have spoken with my mistress about this—I never went to any office to swear to him.
JAMES THOMPSON ( police-constable H 178.) I have known Barney Magnus about four years or more—I do not think he could have been a livery servant without my knowing it.
Cross-examined. Q. Suppose any one had told you to apprehend him, could you have found him? A. Yes.
WILLIAM BURRIDGE . I am a policeman. I produce these coats, which I got from the prosecutrix.
MRS. SAVAGE. These are the coats that were left at my house—I marked them.
JOHN HAMMOND . I am a tailor. These coats have been clothered; they are worth about 22 s.—I have been in business sixteen years.
Cross-examined. Q. What is clothered? A. A coat set off to the best advantage—made to sell and not to wear—no one who was any judge would give seven guineas for these coats, no one would get a bargain by them at 22 s.
GUILTY .* Aged 45.— Transported for Seven Years.
(See Fifth Session, page 839.)
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1108. JAMES RICHARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of March, 8 yards of diaper, value 6 s.; 14 yards of linen-cloth, value 35 s.; 1 handkerchief, value 5 s.; 2 pair of socks, value 1 s.; 1 table cover, value 10 s.; 1 shirt, value 5 s.; and 12 yards of silk, value 32 s.; the goods of William Serjeant and another, his masters.
RICHARD NICHOLSON . I am a draper, in partnership with William Serjeant; the prisoner was in our employ five or six years—I have our book here, in which is entered, in the prisoner's writing, "Mrs. Bennett, Trinity-square, remnant of Irish, 18 s., on approbation;" meaning it was to be left there, and if not approved to be returned; and against it is written "Rd.;" signifying returned—it was not returned; and I asked the prisoner what had become of the Irish that he had taken away a few days before—he said it had been sent to Mrs. Bennett and returned; he had brought it
back himself, and he was willing to satisfy me by going to Mrs. Bennett—I then said, "I had been to Mrs. Bennett"—the prisoner then said, he had taken it and sold it to make up his rent—I went to his premises, in Union-street, where I found this damask and some other articles—I know this damask—I have the pattern at home.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. I believe he stated the circumstances under which he took these things? A. Yes; he said he took it, in consequence of distress—he has a wife and two children—he left our service about six months ago, and applied to be taken back.
(John Morris, of Walnut-tree-walk, Lambeth; Thomas England, a brush-maker, of Union-street; Mr. Price, a cabinet-maker, of New Park-street; and Mr. Webb, a draper, of the Borough; gave the prisoner a good character; the latter of whom promised to find him employ.)
GUILTY. Aged 24.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18350406-1109]
1109. WILLIAM BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of March, 1 basket, value 1 s.; 10 shifts, value 1 s.; 10 night-gowns, value 10 s.; 4 frocks, value 5 s.; 20 napkins, value 14 s.; 11 frocks, value 11 s.; 4 pairs stockings, value 2 s.; 7 pair of socks, value 2 s.; 11 handkerchiefs, value 3 s.; 2 table-cloths, value 5 s.; 7 petticoats, value 10 s.; 7 night-caps, value 3 s.; 1 sheet, value 3 s.; 1 pillow-case, value 6 d.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 2 s.; and 1 dressing-gown, value 5 s.; the goods of Charles Barry: 1 petticoat, value 1 s.; 1 shift, value 1 s.; the goods of Jane Richland: and 1 gown, value 1 s.; 1 petticoat, value 1 s.; and 1 shift, value 9 d.; the goods of Maria Hook; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
MARIA HOOK . I am servant to Mr. Charles Barry, who lives at Camberwell. I packed up these articles; part are my master's and part my own, on the 2nd of March, and placed it in the passage—it was to be washed by Mrs. Brown—her daughter, Hannah, came for it and took it away, to take it to Mr. Brown at Peckham—we have had all the property back.
HANNAH BROWN . My mother washes for Mr. Barry's family—on the 2nd of March, I went to Mr. Barry's for the linen—the prisoner, who is my brother, went with me as far as the gate—I got the linen and took it to the gate, and there the prisoner took it—he carried it, and he told me to go and get him a pipe of tobacco at the Fox—I went, leaving the bundle with him—I returned in five minutes—he was then gone, and the bundle—the bundle was found afterwards in a little shrubbery, near Brockley-Wood—my brother had lived with my mother, but he did not return home.
JAMES WHITE ( police-constable P 171.) I went with another officer in pursuit of the prisoner—I found him on the 8th of March—the linen was found in the wood by two women, who were not bound over, as the linen was returned—I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction, which I got from the Clerk's of the Peace for Surrey—I was a witness, I know the prisoner is the person.
Prisoner's Defence. On the 2nd of March I had no work to do, and I heard that brick-making was stirring down at Erith—I went down, and returned on the 7th of March, and then was accused of this.
GUILTY .* Aged 20.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
